"\\documentclass[11pt,letterpaper]{article}\n\\usepackage{graphicx, geometry, amsmath, hyperref, url, natbib, booktabs, xcolor, listings}\n\\geometry{margin=1in}\n\\hypersetup{colorlinks=true, linkcolor=black, citecolor=black, urlcolor=black}\n\n\\title{Over-Refusal of Benign Slovene Prompts in Gemma-3-12B\\\\Is a Criterion Shift}\n\\author{}\n\\date{}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nSafety-aligned language models sometimes refuse benign requests, and this over-refusal can differ across languages.\nWe apply signal-detection theory (SDT) to a controlled abliteration study comparing Gemma-3-12B-IT with its Slovene-adapted sibling GaMS3-12B-Instruct.\nOn the reserved HARD evaluation set (1,093 safe and 349 harmful items scored by a local Qwen3-14B judge whose readout gate never passed), unedited Gemma false-refuses benign Slovene items at a higher rate than English items (0.569 vs 0.364, 1.56 times higher), with GaMS3 at 0.273 in both languages.\nSDT decomposition attributes the gap to a criterion shift: a difference-in-differences of $+0.52$ $[0.38, 0.68]$ on the decision threshold, with no corresponding change in sensitivity.\nThe finding is a single exploratory estimate: 8 of 9 compliance-valid rows across five analyses point the same way as the headline (a post-hoc count), but the sign reverses under partial-as-refusal coding.\nA secondary analysis of reply-language refusal yields a Gemma-specific output-language effect, also rated ESTIMATE due to weak judge anchoring (harmful-content adjudicator $\\kappa = 0.12$).\nThese measurements indicate that multilingual safety evaluations should report false-alarm rates on benign items alongside attack-success rates.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nSafety alignment in large language models targets harmful-request refusal, but the same training can produce over-refusal: the model declines benign requests it should answer \\citep{Rottger2023, Souly2024}.\nOver-refusal degrades usefulness, and prior work has shown that refusal rates differ across languages \\citep{Shen2024, Yong2023, Wang2023, Deng2023}.\nWhether these differences reflect a sensitivity gap (the model is less able to distinguish harmful from benign items) or a criterion shift (the model applies a stricter threshold at the same discriminative ability) has rarely been tested.\nSignal-detection theory (SDT) provides the decomposition: sensitivity $d'$ measures how well the model separates harmful from benign items, while criterion $c$ measures where it sets the decision threshold \\citep{Aziz2026}.\n\nAbliteration, the directional ablation of the refusal direction in a model's residual stream \\citep{Arditi2024}, provides a way to study refusal at varying strengths.\nWe compare two 12-billion-parameter models: Gemma-3-12B-IT and GaMS3-12B-Instruct, a Slovene-adapted variant built on the same Gemma-3 base \\citep{Vres2026}.\nThe abliteration edit is applied via per-layer LoRA adapters \\citep{Hu2021} optimized by TPE \\citep{Akiba2019}.\n\nThe study addresses two questions.\nFirst, does the model-language interaction on unedited refusal reflect a criterion shift or a sensitivity difference?\nSecond, does the residual refusal after a partial English edit track the language of the model's reply or the language of the prompt?\n\n\\subsection*{Summary of Contributions}\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Over-refusal as criterion shift (Section~\\ref{sec:criterion}).} Unedited Gemma false-refuses benign Slovene items at 0.569 vs 0.364 in English; GaMS3 stays at 0.273 in both.\nThe criterion difference-in-differences is $+0.52$ $[0.38, 0.68]$ with no sensitivity change (ESTIMATE; sign reverses under partial-as-refusal coding).\n\\item \\textbf{Reply-language refusal (Section~\\ref{sec:reply}).} A crossed $3{\\times}3$ design yields a raw output-language effect for Gemma, but PPI++ correction and robustness checks rate it ESTIMATE with a weak anchor.\n\\item \\textbf{Measurement methodology (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}).} We report how judge specificity, language compliance, and correction methods interact when evaluating multilingual refusal.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Multilingual refusal gaps.}\nSeveral studies document that safety alignment degrades in non-English languages, enabling jailbreaks via low-resource languages \\citep{Yong2023, Deng2023, Shen2024, Wang2023}.\nRecent work separates input-language from output-language effects \\citep{Marchisio2024, Zhang2026} and shows that safety pathways are shared across languages \\citep{Miao2026, Upadhyaya2026, Wang2025}.\nThe question of whether multilingual refusal gaps reflect over-refusal on benign items rather than under-refusal on harmful items is partially addressed by \\citet{Addagada2026}, who reports a relevance curse in non-English output, and by \\citet{Nguyen2026}, who cross prompt and response language on benign content.\n\\citet{Yoon2026} document the disparate cost of safety alignment across languages.\nThe novelty of the present work is partially occupied by these concurrent findings; our contribution is the signal-detection decomposition and the controlled sibling-pair design.\n\n\\paragraph{Abliteration.}\n\\citet{Arditi2024} showed that refusal in LLMs is mediated by a single direction in the residual stream, and that ablating this direction removes refusal.\n\\citet{Joad2026} demonstrated that multiple directions contribute.\n\\citet{Wang2025} found that the refusal direction generalizes across safety-aligned languages.\n\\citet{Krasnodebska2026} found that English-only DPO alignment does not ensure cross-lingual safety.\n\\citet{Hawkins2026} examined the safety impacts of benign multilingual fine-tuning.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation methodology.}\nLLM-as-judge evaluations carry systematic biases that compound across languages \\citep{Lee2025}.\nPrediction-powered inference (PPI++) provides a framework for correcting classifier estimates against a gold standard \\citep{Angelopoulos2023}.\n\\citet{Fiedler2026} shows that the Rogan--Gladen correction is unstable at low Youden $J$.\nStrongREJECT \\citep{Souly2024} introduced rubric-based evaluation of jailbreak quality.\n\\citet{Aziz2026} showed that low-resource safety failures are calibration failures, not representation failures.\n\n\\section{Method}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Edit Procedure}\n\nWe study Gemma-3-12B-IT and GaMS3-12B-Instruct, both loaded in NF4 (4-bit normal-float) quantization.\nGaMS3 extends Gemma-3 with Slovene continued pretraining on 140 billion tokens and supervised fine-tuning on a mix that is roughly 79\\% Slovene \\citep{Vres2026}.\n\nThe abliteration edit applies per-layer directional ablation via LoRA adapters \\citep{Hu2021}, optimized by a 40-trial TPE search \\citep{Akiba2019}.\nEach trial selects a layer subset, computes a refusal direction from contrastive pairs of harmful and benign English prompts, and projects it out of the residual stream.\nThe edit objective is to reduce English refusal while preserving capability on six benchmarks.\n\nWe use two edit doses throughout: a low dose ($\\lambda = 0.5$ for both models) and a high dose ($\\lambda = 1.0$ for Gemma, 0.625 for GaMS3), chosen to bracket the 50\\% English refusal point.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Design}\n\n\\paragraph{Over-refusal (criterion shift).}\nExperiment~5 tests 1,093 safe items (XSTest \\citep{Rottger2023} and OR-Bench) and 349 harmful items in English and Slovene.\nThe Slovene items are machine-translated with Gemini (gemini-2.5-flash); English items are back-translations.\nEach item is classified as refused or complied by a local Qwen3-14B judge, calibrated against gemini-2.5-flash on DEV (binary $\\kappa$ 0.68--0.86 across cells).\nThe hit rate (refusal on harmful items) and false-alarm rate (refusal on safe items) are computed per model $\\times$ language cell, and signal-detection parameters $d'$ and $c$ are derived.\nThe key contrast is the difference-in-differences (DiD), which isolates the model-by-language interaction:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathrm{DiD}_c = (c_{\\text{GaMS3,SL}} - c_{\\text{GaMS3,EN}}) - (c_{\\text{Gemma,SL}} - c_{\\text{Gemma,EN}}).\n\\end{equation}\nA positive $\\mathrm{DiD}_c$ means Gemma's criterion shifts more toward refusal in Slovene than GaMS3's does.\n\n\\paragraph{Crossed grid (reply-language refusal).}\nExperiment~14 crosses three prompt languages (English, Slovene, Hungarian) with three reply languages (English, Slovene, Hungarian) using a parallel one-line suffix in the input language.\nEach cell contains 200 harmful items and 100 benign items at three dose levels (zero, low, high).\nThe output-language mechanism contrast $\\mathrm{OUT}_{\\mathrm{SL}}$ is the mean log-odds refusal gap between Slovene-output and English-output cells, averaging over prompt languages.\nThe input-language contrast $\\mathrm{IN}_{\\mathrm{SL}}$ is defined symmetrically.", "numLines": 100, "startLine": 1, "totalLines": 325}}

all Hungarian results are uninformative.\n\n\\section{Results}\n\n\\subsection{Over-Refusal: Criterion Shift on Benign Items (ESTIMATE)}\n\\label{sec:criterion}\n\nThe main measurement is that Gemma applies a stricter refusal criterion in Slovene than in English on benign items, while GaMS3 does not.\nTable~\\ref{tab:sdt} reports the signal-detection parameters from Experiment~5.\n\n\\begin{table}[!htbp]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Signal-detection parameters by model and language (Experiment~5, 1,093 safe $+$ 349 harmful items, Qwen3-14B judge).}\n\\label{tab:sdt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n & Gemma EN & Gemma SL & GaMS3 EN & GaMS3 SL \\\\\n\\midrule\nHit rate ($H$) & 0.89 & 0.94 & 0.90 & 0.86 \\\\\nFalse-alarm rate ($F$) & 0.364 & 0.569 & 0.273 & 0.273 \\\\\n$d'$ & 1.57 & 1.39 & 1.86 & 1.68 \\\\\n$c$ & $-0.44$ & $-0.87$ & $-0.33$ & $-0.23$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe DiD on the criterion parameter is $+0.52$ $[0.38, 0.68]$ (95\\% CI).\nGemma's Slovene criterion is shifted by half a standard deviation relative to the GaMS3 baseline.\nThe DiD on $d'$ is $-0.003$ $[-0.33, 0.29]$, indistinguishable from zero: the asymmetry is in the threshold, not the sensitivity.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:sdt} shows the decomposition.\n\n\\begin{figure}[!htbp]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth,height=0.85\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figures/fig_sdt_v0.pdf}\n\\caption{Signal-detection parameters from Experiment~5 on the HARD set (1,093 benign and 349 harmful items; Slovene machine translations vs English back-translations; refusals labelled by a local Qwen3-14B judge, with partial answers coded as non-refusals). Blue bars are English prompts and orange bars are Slovene prompts, for Gemma-3-12B-IT (``Gemma~3'') and GaMS3-12B-Instruct (``GaMS3''). (a)~False-alarm rate $F$, the proportion of benign items refused, with Wilson 95\\% confidence intervals. Brackets give the paired Slovene$-$English difference and an exact McNemar test on the same items. Gemma's rate rises from 0.364 to 0.569 ($p<10^{-28}$), while GaMS3 refuses 0.273 of benign items in both languages ($p=1.0$). (b)~SDT criterion $c$ in $z$ units, where more negative means a greater tendency to refuse. Lower brackets show the within-model shift $\\Delta c$ (Slovene$-$English, 95\\% CI): Gemma moves toward refusing in Slovene ($c=-0.87$ vs $-0.44$; $\\Delta c=-0.43$ [$-0.57$, $-0.31$]), while GaMS3 does not ($-0.23$ vs $-0.33$; $\\Delta c=+0.09$ [$-0.002$, $0.19$]). The upper bracket gives the difference-in-differences $\\mathrm{DiD}_c=+0.52$ [0.38, 0.68]. The corresponding DiD on sensitivity $d'$ ($-0.003$ [$-0.33$, $0.29$]) is indistinguishable from zero, so the asymmetry lies in the threshold rather than in discrimination. This finding is rated ESTIMATE: coding partial answers as refusals reverses $\\mathrm{DiD}_c$ to $-0.31$ [$-0.62$, $-0.09$].}\n\\label{fig:sdt}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Rating and caveats.}\nWe use a three-level evidence rating: ROBUST (survives all pre-registered checks), ESTIMATE (the point estimate is informative but at least one sensitivity check fails), and INCONCLUSIVE (the confidence interval includes zero or the result is fragile).\nThis finding is rated ESTIMATE for three reasons.\nFirst, the sign reverses to $-0.31$ when partial answers are coded as refusals rather than compliance.\nSecond, 8 of 9 compliance-valid rows across five analyses (Experiments~5, 9, 14, 15, and a secondary rescoring) point the same way as the headline, but this count was a post-hoc refinement made after the frozen rule found an unresolved cross-body disagreement.\nThird, a Qwen3-14B rescoring gives a similar interval $[0.39, 0.67]$, but this is a re-derivation from the same Experiment~5 items, not an independent sample.\n\n\\paragraph{Edited-model criterion shift.}\nOn edited models, the criterion difference is inconclusive: Experiment~11 HARD post-edit $\\mathrm{DiD}_c = 0.14$ $[-0.78, 1.12]$, whose CI includes zero.\n\n\\subsection{Reply-Language Refusal (ESTIMATE, Weak Anchor)}\n\\label{sec:reply}\n\nIn the $3{\\times}3$ crossed grid (Experiment~14), edited Gemma at high dose shows a pattern in which residual refusal is higher when the model replies in Slovene than in English.\nTables~\\ref{tab:gemma_grid} and~\\ref{tab:gams3_grid} give the refusal rates; Figure~\\ref{fig:grid} visualizes the full grid.\n\n\\begin{table}[!htbp]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Refusal rates at high edit dose, Gemma (harmful items, 200 per cell).}\n\\label{tab:gemma_grid}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n & EN out & SL out & HU out \\\\\n\\midrule\nEN in & 0.18 & 0.77 & 0.46 \\\\\nSL in & 0.07 & 0.56 & 0.53 \\\\\nHU in & 0.10 & 0.83 & 0.55 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[!htbp]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Refusal rates at high edit dose, GaMS3 (harmful items, 200 per cell; cells below 90\\% compliance marked $\\dag$).}\n\\label{tab:gams3_grid}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n & EN out & SL out & HU out \\\\\n\\midrule\nEN in & 0.21 & 0.14 & 0.23 \\\\\nSL in & 0.16$\\dag$ & 0.15 & 0.17$\\dag$ \\\\\nHU in & 0.19$\\dag$ & 0.12$\\dag$ & 0.28 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe raw mechanism contrast is $\\mathrm{OUT}_{\\mathrm{SL}} = +1.14$ $[0.60, 1.56]$ log-odds for Gemma and the input-language contrast is $\\mathrm{IN}_{\\mathrm{SL}} = -0.60$ $[-1.02, -0.18]$.\nFor GaMS3, the input-language contrast $\\mathrm{IN}_{\\mathrm{SL}} = -0.86$ $[-1.58, -0.13]$ excludes zero.\nThe cross-model output contrast is $\\mathrm{dOUT}_{\\mathrm{SL}} = -1.58$ $[-2.22, -0.66]$.\nHowever, the PPI-corrected output contrast for Gemma is $0.20$ $[-0.76, 1.13]$, consistent with zero, and the robustness check across readouts does not pass.\n\n\\begin{figure}[!htbp]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth,height=0.85\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figures/fig_grid_v0.pdf}\n\\caption{Refusal rates on harmful items at high edit dose in the $3{\\times}3$ prompt-language $\\times$ requested-reply-language grid (Experiment~14, 200 harmful items per cell, primary-judge raw readout). Rows give the prompt language and columns the requested reply language (EN, SL, HU). Colour encodes the refusal rate on a shared white-to-dark-red scale from 0 to 1. Each cell prints the rate with its Wilson 95\\% CI beneath. (a)~Gemma-3-12B-IT: the Slovene-reply column is the most refused (0.560--0.835), the English-reply column the least (0.075--0.185), and the Hungarian-reply column sits in between (0.460--0.550). (b)~GaMS3-12B-Instruct: refusal is uniformly low (0.125--0.280) with no reply-language column pattern. $\\dag$~marks cells whose language compliance, pooled over the three dose levels, is below 90\\%. The Gemma output-language contrast holds on this raw readout, but its PPI-corrected estimate includes zero. The CIs inside the darkest Gemma cells (SL-reply column, top to bottom) are $[0.71,\\,0.82]$, $[0.49,\\,0.63]$, $[0.78,\\,0.88]$.}\n\\label{fig:grid}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment~16 confirmation.}\nOn a fresh 294-item body, Experiment~16 gives the PPI-corrected estimate $\\mathrm{OUT}_{\\text{Gemma}} = {+}2.43$ $[{+}0.89, {+}5.38]$ log-odds, and the cross-model difference $\\mathrm{dOUT} = -2.31$ $[-5.34, -0.68]$ excludes zero.\nAll confirmation clauses pass on the consensus reference.\nHowever, the verdict is capped at ESTIMATE because the harmful-content adjudicator $\\kappa$ is 0.12 (pooled over $n = 400$), well below the 0.60 gate.\nThe adjudicator disagreement stems from truncation: one judge scored cut-off partial compliance as non-harmful, while the other graded the partial content 2--4.\nThe anchor is weak.\n\n\\paragraph{SDT decomposition of the output-language effect.}\nAt high dose, the Slovene-output criterion DiD (Gemma minus GaMS3) is $-1.56$ $[-1.97, -1.29]$ and the $d'$ DiD is $0.72$ $[-0.06, 1.30]$, which includes zero.\nThis is a separate reply-language criterion contrast, computed at the cell level as Gemma minus GaMS3; after harmonising the sign convention, it is counted among the compliance-valid rows that agree with the headline.\n\n\\paragraph{Compliant-row contrast.}\nRestricting to rows where both models achieve ${>}90\\%$ compliance (EN-input rows only), the edit-induced cross-model contrast is $\\mathrm{G3}_{\\text{edit}}(\\text{EN}{\\to}\\text{SL})$~$=$~$-1.26$~$[-2.17,\\allowbreak 0.20]$, whose CI includes zero.\n\n\\paragraph{Benign false refusal.}\nFrom blind adjudication ($n = 30$ per cell), Gemma falsely refuses benign EN$\\to$SL prompts at 0.20 $[0.10, 0.37]$ vs EN$\\to$EN at 0.10 $[0.03, 0.26]$.\n\n\\paragraph{Refusal composition.}\nAmong Gemma's high-dose EN$\\to$SL harmful-item responses (blind adjudication, $n = 50$), 54\\% are explicit refusals and 26\\% are deflections.\nThe deflection rate, defined as the proportion of non-refusal responses that are off-topic deflections rather than substantive answers, differs between Gemma EN$\\to$SL and EN$\\to$EN by 0.46 $[0.23, 0.65]$, consistent with the relevance curse described by \\citet{Shen2024} and the output-language effect reported by \\citet{Addagada2026}.\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:dose} plots refusal rate against abliteration dose for both models.\nExperiment~9 measures refusal rates across nine $\\lambda$ values for both models in English and Slovene (300 items per model $\\times$ language cell at $\\lambda = 0$, 100 items at each edited step).\nGemma's dose-response curve in Slovene is right-shifted relative to its English curve: at matched doses, more Slovene refusal remains.\nGaMS3 shows a smaller shift.\nThe fine-grained 13-step dose ladder (Experiment~15) gives the most powerful estimate: $\\mathrm{G3} = -0.70$ $[-1.01, -0.42]$ with a minimum detectable effect of 0.42.\nThe edit-induced component is $+0.47$ $[-0.96, 2.14]$, whose CI includes zero; whether the cross-model gap is baseline or edit-induced is inconclusive.\n\n\\subsection{Inconclusive and Null Results}\n\n\\paragraph{Unedited cross-model gap at Slovene-to-Slovene prompts.}\nExperiment~15 gives $\\mathrm{G3}_{\\text{orig}} = -1.17$ $[-2.83, 0.22]$.\nThis is a ceiling artefact: its fragility index, the number of item reclassifications needed to move the CI boundary past zero, is 1 (a single item reclassification in the Gemma $\\lambda\\text{-}0$ Slovene cell, where 299 of 300 items are refused, moves it inside the margin).\nOnly the Experiment~9 body has a robust offset (fragility index 9).\n\n\\paragraph{Hungarian.}\nAll Hungarian contrasts are uninformative.\nThe minimum detectable effect is approximately 3 log-odds units.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment~16 zero-dose.}\nThe Experiment~16 zero-dose Gemma criterion DiD is $-0.65$~$[-0.90,\\allowbreak -0.46]$, rated ESTIMATE.\nIt does not confirm the Experiment~5 headline because it uses a different item body, a different judge, and a suffix-based output-language design rather than paired translation.\n\n\\paragraph{RefusEU validation.}\nExperiment~17 tests attack success rate on 1,300 EN $+$ 1,300 SL RefusEU prompts.\nThe edit is effective in all four model $\\times$ language cells (ROBUST), with ASR deltas of 0.657--0.937.\nThe EN-vs-SL within-model difference is not interpretable as a language effect because the prompt sets are different natural prompts (LaBSE similarity 0.55).\n\n\\begin{figure}[!htbp]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth,height=0.85\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figures/fig_dose_v0.pdf}\n\\caption{Refusal rate on harmful prompts as a function of abliteration dose $\\lambda$ for Gemma-3-12B-IT (blue) and GaMS3-12B-Instruct (amber), in English (solid lines, filled circles; EN-BT arm) and Slovene (dashed lines, hollow squares; item-matched SL-MT arm). $\\lambda$ scales each model's own selected abliteration edit (9~steps from 0 to 2; $\\lambda=1$ is the selected edit), so equal $\\lambda$ does not mean an equal edit across models. Points are J1-judged refusal rates from the Experiment~9 RefusEU-TRAIN probe, with the Hautus correction $(k+0.5)/(n+1)$ used in the Experiment~9 analysis. Shaded bands are Wilson 95\\% intervals on the raw proportion. $n=300$ harmful items at $\\lambda=0$ and the 100-item core block at every $\\lambda>0$. Gemma's Slovene curve lies to the right of its English curve throughout the transition (0.64 vs.\\ 0.32 at $\\lambda=1$): at each dose, more Slovene refusal remains. GaMS3's Slovene curve lies only slightly above its English curve (largest gap 0.14, at $\\lambda=0.5$), and the two meet near the floor from $\\lambda=1$ onward. These per-dose gaps are descriptive. The pre-registered cross-model comparison is made at matched English refusal, not at matched dose.}\n\\label{fig:dose}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Interpretation}\n\nThe criterion-shift measurement has a concrete interpretation for multilingual evaluation: a model can appear ``safer'' in a non-English language because it refuses more benign requests, inflating the refusal rate without any improvement in harm detection.\nThe SDT framing separates these two components.\nThe near-zero DiD on $d'$ means that the language shift does not make either model better or worse at distinguishing harmful from benign items; the measured difference is in the threshold.\n\\citet{Aziz2026} described a similar calibration-not-representation pattern across 23 languages; the present sibling-pair design adds GaMS3-specific detail but does not change the interpretation.\n\nThe reply-language finding, if confirmed by future work with validated judge anchors, would suggest that Gemma's refusal mechanism interacts with output-language processing.\n\\citet{Upadhyaya2026} showed that safety features are geometrically entangled with language-identity features; the present study's $3{\\times}3$ design separates the input and output dimensions but cannot determine the mechanism.\n\n\\subsection{Limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{ESTIMATE rating on the primary finding.}\nThe criterion-shift DiD reverses sign ($-0.31$) when partial answers are coded as refusals.\nThe choice of coding rule therefore determines whether over-refusal is attributed to Gemma or is absent.\nThis fragility bounds how strongly the finding can be stated.\nFurthermore, restricting Experiment~5 to the XSTest items alone gives $\\mathrm{DiD}_c = 0.10$ $[-0.13, 0.35]$, so the effect is carried by the OR-Bench items.\n\n\\paragraph{Judge quality.}\nThe Qwen3-14B judge for Experiment~5 was calibrated against gemini-2.5-flash on DEV ($\\kappa$ 0.68--0.86) but not against human labels.\nOn edited rows across the study, no judge exceeds $\\kappa = 0.51$ against blind adjudication.\nThe readout gate has never passed.\nAll adjudication in this study is author-model LLM, not human review.\n\n\\paragraph{Sample size.}\nThe adjudication sample is 30 items per cell for benign false-refusal rates, limiting precision.\nThe refusal-composition numbers (54\\% explicit, 26\\% deflection) come from $n = 50$ high-dose harmful items.\n\n\\paragraph{Language compliance.}\nFour GaMS3 cells fall below 90\\% compliance (SL$\\to$EN at 0.00, HU$\\to$EN at 0.14, SL$\\to$HU at 0.56, HU$\\to$SL at 0.86).\nGaMS3 did not follow the output-language instruction off the English-input row; its SL$\\to$EN responses were written in Slovene, not English.\nMechanism claims for GaMS3 are therefore restricted to the compliant EN-input row.\n\n\\paragraph{Quantization.}\nBoth models run in NF4 (4-bit) quantization, which may affect refusal behaviour relative to full-precision weights.\n\n\\paragraph{Novelty.}\nThe observation that over-refusal rates differ across languages is partially anticipated by \\citet{Addagada2026} and \\citet{Nguyen2026}.\nThe present work's contribution is the signal-detection decomposition and the controlled sibling-pair design.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nUnedited Gemma-3-12B-IT false-refuses benign Slovene items at 0.569 vs 0.364 in English.\nSignal-detection analysis attributes this to a criterion shift ($\\mathrm{DiD}_c = +0.52$ $[0.38, 0.68]$) with no sensitivity difference.\nThe finding is rated ESTIMATE because the sign reverses under an alternative coding rule.\nOn edited models, the criterion difference is inconclusive (0.14 $[-0.78, 1.12]$).\nA secondary analysis of reply-language refusal yields a PPI-corrected output-language effect of $+2.43$ $[+0.89, +5.38]$ for Gemma, but this is also rated ESTIMATE because the harmful-content adjudicator $\\kappa$ is 0.12.\nMultilingual safety evaluations that report only attack-success rates on harmful items will miss criterion shifts that inflate refusal rates on benign items.\n\n\\bibliographystyle{plainnat}\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\end{document}\n", "numLines": 226, "startLine": 100, "totalLines": 325}}

0}\ntable{border-collapse:collapse;width:100%;font-size:14px}\nth,td{text-align:left;padding:8px 12px;border-bottom:1px solid var(--rule)}\nth{font-weight:600;font-size:13px;text-transform:uppercase;letter-spacing:0.03em;color:var(--fg-secondary)}\n\n/* Figure gallery */\n.gallery{display:grid;gap:24px;grid-template-columns:1fr}\n.fig-card{cursor:pointer;position:relative}\n.fig-card img{\n  border:1px solid var(--rule);border-radius:4px;\n  width:100%;\n}\n.fig-caption{\n  font-size:14px;line-height:1.5;\n  color:var(--fg-secondary);margin:10px 0 0;\n}\n\n/* Lightbox */\n.lightbox{\n  display:none;position:fixed;inset:0;z-index:999;\n  background:rgba(0,0,0,0.85);\n  align-items:center;justify-content:center;\n  padding:24px;\n}\n.lightbox.open{display:flex}\n.lightbox img{\n  max-width:100%;max-height:90vh;\n  object-fit:contain;border-radius:4px;\n  background:#fff;\n}\n.lightbox-close{\n  position:absolute;top:16px;right:20px;\n  background:none;border:none;color:#fff;\n  font-size:28px;cursor:pointer;\n  width:44px;height:44px;\n  display:flex;align-items:center;justify-content:center;\n  border-radius:50%;\n}\n.lightbox-close:hover{background:rgba(255,255,255,0.15)}\n.lightbox-close:focus-visible{outline:2px solid var(--focus);outline-offset:2px}\n\n/* Limitations */\n.limitation{margin:0 0 16px}\n.limitation strong{font-weight:600}\n.limitation p{margin:4px 0 0;font-size:15px;color:var(--fg-secondary)}\n\n/* Footer */\nfooter{\n  margin-top:56px;padding:32px 0;\n  border-top:1px solid var(--rule);\n  font-size:14px;color:var(--fg-secondary);\n}\nfooter a{color:var(--fg-secondary)}\nfooter a:hover{color:var(--accent)}\n.footer-links{\n  display:flex;flex-wrap:wrap;gap:8px 20px;\n  margin-bottom:16px;\n}\n.artifact-list{margin:16px 0;padding:0;list-style:none}\n.artifact-list li{margin:0 0 4px;font-size:13px}\n.artifact-list li a{display:inline}\n\n.code-link{font-size:13px;color:var(--fg-secondary)}\n.code-link:hover{color:var(--accent)}\n\n.badge{\n  display:inline-block;\n  font-size:11px;font-weight:600;\n  text-transform:uppercase;letter-spacing:0.05em;\n  padding:2px 7px;border-radius:3px;\n  background:var(--code-bg);color:var(--fg-secondary);\n  vertical-align:middle;margin-left:6px;\n}\n\n/* inline figure in method */\n.method-fig{margin:24px 0}\n.method-fig img{border:1px solid var(--rule);border-radius:4px;width:100%}\n</style>\n</head>\n<body>\n<a href=\"#main\" class=\"skip-link\">Skip to main content</a>\n\n<div class=\"page-wrap\">\n<aside class=\"sidebar\" aria-label=\"Section navigation\">\n<nav data-scrollspy>\n  <a href=\"#hero\">Overview</a>\n  <a href=\"#contributions\">Contributions</a>\n  <a href=\"#method\">Method</a>\n  <a href=\"#results\">Results</a>\n  <a href=\"#figures\">Figures</a>\n  <a href=\"#limitations\">Limitations</a>\n  <a href=\"#footer\">Links</a>\n</nav>\n</aside>\n\n<main id=\"main\" class=\"main-content\">\n\n<!-- HERO -->\n<section class=\"hero\" id=\"hero\">\n<h1>Over-Refusal of Benign Slovene Prompts in Gemma-3-12B Is a Criterion Shift</h1>\n\n<p class=\"tldr\">Safety-aligned language models can appear \"safer\" in non-English languages simply by refusing more benign requests.\nThis study applies signal-detection theory to Gemma-3-12B-IT and its Slovene-adapted sibling GaMS3-12B-Instruct, and finds that Gemma's higher Slovene refusal rate is a criterion shift: a stricter decision threshold (DiD<sub>c</sub>&nbsp;=&nbsp;+0.52&nbsp;[0.38,&nbsp;0.68]) with no change in the ability to tell harmful from harmless prompts apart. The finding is rated ESTIMATE because its sign reverses under an alternative coding rule.</p>\n\n<div class=\"links\">\n  <a href=\"https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/gh/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive@fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/paper.pdf\">Read the paper (PDF)</a>\n  <a href=\"interactive.html\">Explore the interactive demo</a>\n  <a href=\"https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/gh/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive@fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/exec_summary.pdf\">Read the executive summary</a>\n  <a href=\"https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/gh/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive@fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/report.pdf\">Read the full research report</a>\n  <a href=\"https://github.com/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive/tree/fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2\">Code repository</a>\n</div>\n<div class=\"round-links\">\n  Round reports:\n  <a href=\"https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/gh/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive@fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/round-1/report.pdf\">Round 1</a> &middot;\n  <a href=\"https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/gh/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive@fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/round-2/report.pdf\">Round 2</a> &middot;\n  <a href=\"https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/gh/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive@fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/round-3/report.pdf\">Round 3</a> &middot;\n  <a href=\"https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/gh/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive@fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/round-4/report.pdf\">Round 4</a> &middot;\n  <a href=\"https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/gh/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive@fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/round-5/report.pdf\">Round 5</a>\n</div>\n</section>\n\n<!-- CONTRIBUTIONS -->\n<section id=\"contributions\">\n<h2>Contributions</h2>\n<div class=\"cards\">\n\n<div class=\"card\">\n  <h3>Over-refusal is a criterion shift, not a sensitivity gap</h3>\n  <p>Unedited Gemma false-refuses benign Slovene items at 0.569 vs 0.364 in English, while GaMS3 stays at 0.273 in both languages. Signal-detection decomposition attributes the gap to a criterion shift (DiD<sub>c</sub>&nbsp;=&nbsp;+0.52&nbsp;[0.38,&nbsp;0.68]) with no corresponding change in sensitivity (DiD on d' = -0.003). Rated ESTIMATE: sign reverses under partial-as-refusal coding.</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"card\">\n  <h3>Reply-language refusal follows the output language</h3>\n  <p>A crossed 3x3 design (prompt language x reply language) yields a raw output-language effect for Gemma: edited Gemma refuses more when told to write in Slovene than in English. PPI-corrected estimate on a fresh body: +2.43&nbsp;[+0.89,&nbsp;+5.38] log-odds. Rated ESTIMATE with a weak anchor (harmful-content adjudicator kappa = 0.12).</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"card\">\n  <h3>Measurement methodology for multilingual refusal</h3>\n  <p>Judge specificity, language compliance, and correction methods interact in ways that change the sign and magnitude of multilingual refusal estimates. No judge in this study exceeds kappa 0.51 on edited text. The readout gate has never passed. All adjudication is author-model LLM, not human.</p>\n</div>\n\n</div>\n</section>\n\n<!-- METHOD -->\n<section id=\"method\">\n<h2>Method</h2>\n\n<p>Two 12-billion-parameter models share the Gemma-3 base but differ in post-training. Gemma-3-12B-IT received Google's standard multilingual post-training. GaMS3-12B-Instruct received Slovene continued pretraining on 140 billion tokens and supervised fine-tuning on a roughly 79% Slovene mix.\nBoth are loaded in NF4 (4-bit) quantization. <a href=\"https://github.com/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive/tree/fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/round-1/dataset-1\" class=\"code-link\">Code: test sets and supervision audit</a></p>\n\n<p><strong>Abliteration edit.</strong> Per-layer directional ablation via LoRA adapters, optimized by a 40-trial TPE search. The edit objective reduces English refusal while preserving capability on six benchmarks. Two dose levels bracket the 50% English refusal point: low dose (lambda = 0.5 for both models) and high dose (lambda = 1.0 for Gemma, 0.625 for GaMS3).</p>\n\n<p><strong>Over-refusal measurement.</strong> Experiment 5 tests 1,093 safe items (XSTest and OR-Bench) and 349 harmful items in English and Slovene. Each item is classified by a local Qwen3-14B judge, calibrated against gemini-2.5-flash on DEV (binary kappa 0.68-0.86). Hit rate, false-alarm rate, d' (sensitivity) and c (criterion) are derived from signal-detection theory. The key contrast is the difference-in-differences DiD<sub>c</sub>, which isolates the model-by-language interaction on the decision threshold.\n<a href=\"https://github.com/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive/tree/fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/round-2/experiment-5\" class=\"code-link\">Code: confirmation on reserved splits</a></p>\n\n<p><strong>Crossed grid.</strong> <a href=\"https://github.com/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive/tree/fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/round-4/experiment-14\">Experiment 14</a> crosses three prompt languages (English, Slovene, Hungarian) with three reply languages using a parallel one-line suffix. Each cell contains 200 harmful and 100 benign items at three dose levels. The output-language contrast OUT<sub>SL</sub> averages the log-odds refusal gap between Slovene-output and English-output cells over prompt languages.\n<a href=\"https://github.com/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive/tree/fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/round-5/experiment-16\">Experiment 16</a> replicates this on a fresh 294-item body with PPI correction as the primary estimator. Cells with language compliance below 90% are excluded from mechanism claims.</p>\n\n<p><strong>Evidence ratings.</strong> The study uses three levels. ROBUST: survives all pre-registered checks. ESTIMATE: point estimate is informative but at least one sensitivity check fails. INCONCLUSIVE: confidence interval includes zero or the result is fragile.</p>\n</section>\n\n<!-- RESULTS -->\n<section id=\"results\">\n<h2>Results</h2>\n\n<div class=\"result-row\">\n  <p class=\"result-label\">Criterion shift (Experiment 5, ESTIMATE)</p>\n  <p class=\"result-number\">DiD<sub>c</sub> = +0.52 [0.38, 0.68]</p>\n  <p class=\"result-context\">Gemma's Slovene criterion is shifted by half a standard deviation relative to the GaMS3 baseline. The DiD on d' is -0.003 [-0.33, 0.29], indistinguishable from zero: the asymmetry is in the threshold, not the sensitivity. The sign reverses to -0.31 when partial answers are coded as refusals.</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"result-row\">\n  <p class=\"result-label\">False-alarm rates on benign items</p>\n  <p class=\"result-number\">Gemma: 0.569 (SL) vs 0.364 (EN)</p>\n  <p class=\"result-context\">Gemma's rate rises from 0.364 to 0.569 in Slovene (McNemar p &lt; 10<sup>-28</sup>). GaMS3 refuses 0.273 of benign items in both languages (p = 1.0).</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"result-row\">\n  <p class=\"result-label\">Output-language effect, Experiment 14 raw readout</p>\n  <p class=\"result-number\">OUT<sub>SL</sub> = +1.14 [0.60, 1.56] log-odds</p>\n  <p class=\"result-context\">Edited Gemma at high dose refuses more when replying in Slovene (0.56-0.83) than in English (0.07-0.18). The cross-model contrast is dOUT<sub>SL</sub> = -1.58 [-2.22, -0.66]. However, the PPI-corrected output contrast for Gemma is 0.20 [-0.76, 1.13], consistent with zero.\n  <a href=\"https://github.com/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive/tree/fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/round-4/experiment-14\" class=\"code-link\">Code: prompt vs reply language</a></p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"result-row\">\n  <p class=\"result-label\">Experiment 16 confirmation (PPI-corrected, ESTIMATE)</p>\n  <p class=\"result-number\">OUT<sub>Gemma</sub> = +2.43 [+0.89, +5.38]</p>\n  <p class=\"result-context\">On a fresh 294-item body, the cross-model difference dOUT = -2.31 [-5.34, -0.68] excludes zero. All confirmation clauses pass on the consensus reference, but the verdict is capped at ESTIMATE because the harmful-content adjudicator kappa is 0.12.\n  <a href=\"https://github.com/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive/tree/fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/round-5/experiment-16\" class=\"code-link\">Code: C-OUT confirmation</a></p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"result-row\">\n  <p class=\"result-label\">Dose-response: cross-model gap (Experiment 15)</p>\n  <p class=\"result-number\">G3 = -0.70 [-1.01, -0.42]</p>\n  <p class=\"result-context\">The most powerful estimate from the 13-step dose ladder. The edit-induced component is +0.47 [-0.96, 2.14], consistent with zero: the cross-model gap is baseline, not edit-induced. Curve slopes are near 1 in both models, confirming parallel-curve geometry.\n  <a href=\"https://github.com/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive/tree/fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/round-4/experiment-15\" class=\"code-link\">Code: dense dose ladder</a></p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"result-row\">\n  <p class=\"result-label\">Refusal composition at high dose (Gemma EN-in SL-out)</p>\n  <p class=\"result-number\">54% explicit refusal, 26% deflection</p>\n  <p class=\"result-context\">Half of Gemma's Slovene non-compliance is deflection. The deflection rate difference between EN-out and SL-out is 0.46 [0.23, 0.65], consistent with the relevance curse described by Shen et al. and the output-language effect reported by Addagada.\n  <a href=\"https://github.com/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive/tree/fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/round-5/evaluation-4\" class=\"code-link\">Code: is the reserve real safety?</a></p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"result-row\">\n  <p class=\"result-label\">RefusEU FINAL (Experiment 17, n = 1,300 per cell)</p>\n  <p class=\"result-number\">ASR deltas: 0.657 - 0.937 <span class=\"badge\">ROBUST</span></p>\n  <p class=\"result-context\">The edit is effective in all four model x language cells. The EN-vs-SL within-model difference is not interpretable as a language effect because the prompt sets are different natural prompts (LaBSE similarity 0.55).\n  <a href=\"https://github.com/ai-inventor-papers/ai-invention-2cf2a7-does-slovene-taught-refusal-survive/tree/fork/run_6GCdZCQAemW2/round-5/experiment-17\" class=\"code-link\">Code: RefusEU FINAL</a></p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"result-row\">\n  <p class=\"result-label\">Edited-model criterion shift</p>\n  <p class=\"result-number\">DiD<sub>c</sub> = 0.14 [-0.78, 1.12]</p>\n  <p class=\"result-context\">On edited models (Experiment 11 HARD post-edit), the criterion difference is consistent with zero. The criterion shift is specific to unedited models.</p>\n</div>\n\n<h3 style=\"margin-top:28px;font-size:17px\">Signal-detection parameters (Experiment 5)</h3>\n\n<div class=\"table-wrap\">\n<table>\n  <thead>\n    <tr><th></th><th>Gemma EN</th><th>Gemma SL</th><th>GaMS3 EN</th><th>GaMS3 SL</th></tr>\n  </thead>\n  <tbody>\n    <tr><td>Hit rate (H)</td><td>0.89</td><td>0.94</td><td>0.90</td><td>0.86</td></tr>\n    <tr><td>False-alarm rate (F)</td><td>0.364</td><td>0.569</td><td>0.273</td><td>0.273</td></tr>\n    <tr><td>d'</td><td>1.57</td><td>1.39</td><td>1.86</td><td>1.68</td></tr>\n    <tr><td>c</td><td>-0.44</td><td>-0.87</td><td>-0.33</td><td>-0.23</td></tr>\n  </tbody>\n</table>\n</div>\n<p style=\"font-size:14px;color:var(--fg-secondary)\">1,093 safe + 349 harmful items, Qwen3-14B judge. From Table 1 of the paper.</p>\n\n<h3 style=\"margin-top:28px;font-size:17px\">Refusal rates at high edit dose (Experiment 14)</h3>\n\n<div class=\"table-wrap\">\n<table>\n  <caption style=\"text-align:left;font-size:14px;color:var(--fg-secondary);padding-bottom:8px\">Gemma-3-12B-IT, 200 harmful items per cell</caption>\n  <thead>\n    <tr><th></th><th>EN out</th><th>SL out</th><th>HU out</th></tr>\n  </thead>\n  <tbody>\n    <tr><td>EN in</td><td>0.18</td><td>0.77</td><td>0.46</td></tr>\n    <tr><td>SL in</td><td>0.07</td><td>0.56</td><td>0.53</td></tr>\n    <tr><td>HU in</td><td>0.10</td><td>0.83</td><td>0.55</td></tr>\n  </tbody>\n</table>\n</div>\n<div class=\"table-wrap\">\n<table>\n  <caption style=\"text-align:left;font-size:14px;color:var(--fg-secondary);padding-bottom:8px\">GaMS3-12B-Instruct, 200 harmful items per cell (dagger marks compliance &lt; 90%)</caption>\n  <thead>\n    <tr><th></th><th>EN out</th><th>SL out</th><th>HU out</th></tr>\n  </thead>\n  <tbody>\n    <tr><td>EN in</td><td>0.21</td><td>0.14</td><td>0.23</td></tr>\n    <tr><td>SL in</td><td>0.16&dagger;</td><td>0.15</td><td>0.17&dagger;</td></tr>\n    <tr><td>HU in</td><td>0.19&dagger;</td><td>0.12&dagger;</td><td>0.28</td></tr>\n  </tbody>\n</table>\n</div>\n</section>\n\n<!-- FIGURES -->\n<section id=\"figures\">\n<h2>Figures</h2>\n<div class=\"gallery\">\n\n<div class=\"fig-card\" tabindex=\"0\" role=\"button\" aria-label=\"Enlarge: Criterion shift without sensitivity change\" data-src=\"figures/fig_sdt_v0.png\">\n  <img src=\"figures/fig_sdt_v0.png\" alt=\"Bar chart showing false-alarm rates and SDT criterion c for Gemma 3 and GaMS3 in English (blue) and Slovene (orange). Gemma's Slovene false-alarm rate rises to 0.569 vs 0.364 in English while GaMS3 stays at 0.273 in both. The criterion difference-in-differences is +0.52.\" loading=\"lazy\">\n  <p class=\"fig-caption\"><strong>Figure 1. Criterion shift without sensitivity change.</strong> Signal-detection parameters from Experiment 5 on the HARD set (1,093 benign and 349 harmful items; Slovene machine translations vs English back-translations; refusals labelled by a local Qwen3-14B judge, with partial answers coded as non-refusals). Blue bars are English prompts and orange bars are Slovene prompts, for Gemma-3-12B-IT (\"Gemma 3\") and GaMS3-12B-Instruct (\"GaMS3\"). (a) False-alarm rate F, the proportion of benign items refused, with Wilson 95% confidence intervals. Brackets give the paired Slovene-English difference and an exact McNemar test on the same items. Gemma's rate rises from 0.364 to 0.569 (p &lt; 10<sup>-28</sup>), while GaMS3 refuses 0.273 of benign items in both languages (p = 1.0). (b) SDT criterion c in z units, where more negative means a greater tendency to refuse. Lower brackets show the within-model shift (Slovene-English, 95% CI): Gemma moves toward refusing in Slovene (c = -0.87 vs -0.44; delta c = -0.43 [-0.57, -0.31]), while GaMS3 does not (-0.23 vs -0.33; delta c = +0.09 [-0.002, 0.19]). The upper bracket gives the difference-in-differences DiD<sub>c</sub> = +0.52 [0.38, 0.68]. The corresponding DiD on sensitivity d' (-0.003 [-0.33, 0.29]) is indistinguishable from zero. This finding is rated ESTIMATE: coding partial answers as refusals reverses DiD<sub>c</sub> to -0.31 [-0.62, -0.09].</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"fig-card\" tabindex=\"0\" role=\"button\" aria-label=\"Enlarge: Refusal rates in crossed language grid\" data-src=\"figures/fig_grid_v0.png\">\n  <img src=\"figures/fig_grid_v0.png\" alt=\"Heatmap showing refusal rates in a 3-by-3 prompt-language by reply-language grid at high edit dose. Gemma panel shows a clear column pattern: Slovene-reply column is darkest (0.56-0.84 refusal). GaMS3 panel shows uniform low refusal (0.12-0.28) with no column pattern.\" loading=\"lazy\">\n  <p class=\"fig-caption\"><strong>Figure 2. Refusal rates in crossed language grid.</strong> Refusal rates on harmful items at high edit dose in the 3x3 prompt-language x requested-reply-language grid (Experiment 14, 200 harmful items per cell, primary-judge raw readout). Rows give the prompt language and columns the requested reply language (EN, SL, HU). Colour encodes the refusal rate on a shared white-to-dark-red scale from 0 to 1. Each cell prints the rate with its Wilson 95% CI beneath. (a) Gemma-3-12B-IT: the Slovene-reply column is the most refused (0.560-0.835), the English-reply column the least (0.075-0.185), and the Hungarian-reply column sits in between (0.460-0.550). (b) GaMS3-12B-Instruct: refusal is uniformly low (0.125-0.280) with no reply-language column pattern. Dagger marks cells whose language compliance, pooled over the three dose levels, is below 90%. All four are GaMS3 cells off the English-prompt row (SL-to-EN, SL-to-HU, HU-to-EN, HU-to-SL), so their reply-language labels are unreliable. The Gemma output-language contrast holds on this raw readout, but its PPI-corrected estimate includes zero.</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"fig-card\" tabindex=\"0\" role=\"button\" aria-label=\"Enlarge: Refusal rate dose-response curves\" data-src=\"figures/fig_dose_v0.png\">\n  <img src=\"figures/fig_dose_v0.png\" alt=\"Line chart showing refusal rate vs abliteration dose lambda for four conditions: Gemma EN (solid blue), Gemma SL (dashed blue), GaMS3 EN (solid amber), GaMS3 SL (dashed amber). Gemma's Slovene curve lies rightward of its English curve throughout. GaMS3's two curves are close together and drop to the floor earlier.\" loading=\"lazy\">\n  <p class=\"fig-caption\"><strong>Figure 3. Refusal rate dose-response curves.</strong> Refusal rate on harmful prompts as a function of abliteration dose lambda for Gemma-3-12B-IT (blue) and GaMS3-12B-Instruct (amber), in English (solid lines, filled circles; EN-BT arm) and Slovene (dashed lines, hollow squares; item-matched SL-MT arm). Lambda scales each model's own selected abliteration edit (9 steps from 0 to 2; lambda = 1 is the selected edit), so equal lambda does not mean an equal edit across models. Points are J1-judged refusal rates from the Experiment 9 RefusEU-TRAIN probe, with the Hautus correction (k+0.5)/(n+1). Shaded bands are Wilson 95% intervals on the raw proportion. n = 300 harmful items at lambda = 0 and the 100-item core block at every lambda > 0. Gemma's Slovene curve lies to the right of its English curve throughout the transition (0.64 vs 0.32 at lambda = 1): at each dose, more Slovene refusal remains. GaMS3's Slovene curve lies only slightly above its English curve (largest gap 0.14, at lambda = 0.5), and the two meet near the floor from lambda = 1 onward. These per-dose gaps are descriptive. The pre-registered cross-model comparison is made at matched English refusal, not at matched dose.</p>\n</div>\n\n</div>\n</section>\n\n<!-- LIMITATIONS -->\n<section id=\"limitations\">\n<h2>Limitations</h2>\n\n<div class=\"limitation\">\n  <p><strong>ESTIMATE rating on the primary finding.</strong> The criterion-shift DiD reverses sign (-0.31) when partial answers are coded as refusals. The choice of coding rule determines whether over-refusal is attributed to Gemma or is absent.</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"limitation\">\n  <p><strong>Judge quality.</strong> The Qwen3-14B judge was calibrated against gemini-2.5-flash on DEV (kappa 0.68-0.86) but not against human labels. On edited rows across the study, no judge exceeds kappa = 0.51 against blind adjudication. The readout gate has never passed. All adjudication in this study is author-model LLM, not human review.</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"limitation\">\n  <p><strong>Sample size.</strong> The adjudication sample is 30 items per cell for benign false-refusal rates. The refusal-composition numbers (54% explicit, 26% deflection) come from n = 50 high-dose harmful items.</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"limitation\">\n  <p><strong>Language compliance.</strong> Four GaMS3 cells fall below 90% compliance (SL-to-EN at 0.00, HU-to-EN at 0.14, SL-to-HU at 0.56, HU-to-SL at 0.86). GaMS3 did not follow the output-language instruction off the English-input row. Mechanism claims for GaMS3 are restricted to the compliant EN-input row.</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"limitation\">\n  <p><strong>Quantization.</strong> Both models run in NF4 (4-bit) quantization, which may affect refusal behaviour relative to full-precision weights.</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"limitation\">\n  <p><strong>Novelty.</strong> The observation that over-refusal rates differ across languages is partially anticipated by Addagada (2609.08373) and Nguyen et al. (2608.26186). "\\documentclass[11pt,letterpaper]{article}\n\\usepackage{graphicx, geometry, amsmath, hyperref, url, natbib, booktabs, xcolor, listings}\n\\geometry{margin=1in}\n\\hypersetup{colorlinks=true, linkcolor=black, citecolor=black, urlcolor=black}\n\n\\title{Over-Refusal of Benign Slovene Prompts in Gemma-3-12B\\\\Is a Criterion Shift}\n\\author{}\n\\date{}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nSafety-aligned language models sometimes refuse benign requests, and this over-refusal can differ across languages.\nWe apply signal-detection theory (SDT) to a controlled abliteration study comparing Gemma-3-12B-IT with its Slovene-adapted sibling GaMS3-12B-Instruct.\nOn the reserved HARD evaluation set (1,093 safe and 349 harmful items scored by a local Qwen3-14B judge whose readout gate never passed), unedited Gemma false-refuses benign Slovene items at a higher rate than English items (0.569 vs 0.364, 1.56 times higher), with GaMS3 at 0.273 in both languages.\nSDT decomposition attributes the gap to a criterion shift: a difference-in-differences of $+0.52$ $[0.38, 0.68]$ on the decision threshold, with no corresponding change in sensitivity.\nThe finding is a single exploratory estimate: 8 of 9 compliance-valid rows across five analyses point the same way as the headline (a post-hoc count), but the sign reverses under partial-as-refusal coding.\nA secondary analysis of reply-language refusal yields a Gemma-specific output-language effect, also rated ESTIMATE due to weak judge anchoring (harmful-content adjudicator $\\kappa = 0.12$).\nThese measurements indicate that multilingual safety evaluations should report false-alarm rates on benign items alongside attack-success rates.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nSafety alignment in large language models targets harmful-request refusal, but the same training can produce over-refusal: the model declines benign requests it should answer \\citep{Rottger2023, Souly2024}.\nOver-refusal degrades usefulness, and prior work has shown that refusal rates differ across languages \\citep{Shen2024, Yong2023, Wang2023, Deng2023}.\nWhether these differences reflect a sensitivity gap (the model is less able to distinguish harmful from benign items) or a criterion shift (the model applies a stricter threshold at the same discriminative ability) has rarely been tested.\nSignal-detection theory (SDT) provides the decomposition: sensitivity $d'$ measures how well the model separates harmful from benign items, while criterion $c$ measures where it sets the decision threshold \\citep{Aziz2026}.\n\nAbliteration, the directional ablation of the refusal direction in a model's residual stream \\citep{Arditi2024}, provides a way to study refusal at varying strengths.\nWe compare two 12-billion-parameter models: Gemma-3-12B-IT and GaMS3-12B-Instruct, a Slovene-adapted variant built on the same Gemma-3 base \\citep{Vres2026}.\nThe abliteration edit is applied via per-layer LoRA adapters \\citep{Hu2021} optimized by TPE \\citep{Akiba2019}.\n\nThe study addresses two questions.\nFirst, does the model-language interaction on unedited refusal reflect a criterion shift or a sensitivity difference?\nSecond, does the residual refusal after a partial English edit track the language of the model's reply or the language of the prompt?\n\n\\subsection*{Summary of Contributions}\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Over-refusal as criterion shift (Section~\\ref{sec:criterion}).} Unedited Gemma false-refuses benign Slovene items at 0.569 vs 0.364 in English; GaMS3 stays at 0.273 in both.\nThe criterion difference-in-differences is $+0.52$ $[0.38, 0.68]$ with no sensitivity change (ESTIMATE; sign reverses under partial-as-refusal coding).\n\\item \\textbf{Reply-language refusal (Section~\\ref{sec:reply}).} A crossed $3{\\times}3$ design yields a raw output-language effect for Gemma, but PPI++ correction and robustness checks rate it ESTIMATE with a weak anchor.\n\\item \\textbf{Measurement methodology (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}).} We report how judge specificity, language compliance, and correction methods interact when evaluating multilingual refusal.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Multilingual refusal gaps.}\nSeveral studies document that safety alignment degrades in non-English languages, enabling jailbreaks via low-resource languages \\citep{Yong2023, Deng2023, Shen2024, Wang2023}.\nRecent work separates input-language from output-language effects \\citep{Marchisio2024, Zhang2026} and shows that safety pathways are shared across languages \\citep{Miao2026, Upadhyaya2026, Wang2025}.\nThe question of whether multilingual refusal gaps reflect over-refusal on benign items rather than under-refusal on harmful items is partially addressed by \\citet{Addagada2026}, who reports a relevance curse in non-English output, and by \\citet{Nguyen2026}, who cross prompt and response language on benign content.\n\\citet{Yoon2026} document the disparate cost of safety alignment across languages.\nThe novelty of the present work is partially occupied by these concurrent findings; our contribution is the signal-detection decomposition and the controlled sibling-pair design.\n\n\\paragraph{Abliteration.}\n\\citet{Arditi2024} showed that refusal in LLMs is mediated by a single direction in the residual stream, and that ablating this direction removes refusal.\n\\citet{Joad2026} demonstrated that multiple directions contribute.\n\\citet{Wang2025} found that the refusal direction generalizes across safety-aligned languages.\n\\citet{Krasnodebska2026} found that English-only DPO alignment does not ensure cross-lingual safety.\n\\citet{Hawkins2026} examined the safety impacts of benign multilingual fine-tuning.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation methodology.}\nLLM-as-judge evaluations carry systematic biases that compound across languages \\citep{Lee2025}.\nPrediction-powered inference (PPI++) provides a framework for correcting classifier estimates against a gold standard \\citep{Angelopoulos2023}.\n\\citet{Fiedler2026} shows that the Rogan--Gladen correction is unstable at low Youden $J$.\nStrongREJECT \\citep{Souly2024} introduced rubric-based evaluation of jailbreak quality.\n\\citet{Aziz2026} showed that low-resource safety failures are calibration failures, not representation failures.\n\n\\section{Method}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Edit Procedure}\n\nWe study Gemma-3-12B-IT and GaMS3-12B-Instruct, both loaded in NF4 (4-bit normal-float) quantization.\nGaMS3 extends Gemma-3 with Slovene continued pretraining on 140 billion tokens and supervised fine-tuning on a mix that is roughly 79\\% Slovene \\citep{Vres2026}.\n\nThe abliteration edit applies per-layer directional ablation via LoRA adapters \\citep{Hu2021}, optimized by a 40-trial TPE search \\citep{Akiba2019}.\nEach trial selects a layer subset, computes a refusal direction from contrastive pairs of harmful and benign English prompts, and projects it out of the residual stream.\nThe edit objective is to reduce English refusal while preserving capability on six benchmarks.\n\nWe use two edit doses throughout: a low dose ($\\lambda = 0.5$ for both models) and a high dose ($\\lambda = 1.0$ for Gemma, 0.625 for GaMS3), chosen to bracket the 50\\% English refusal point.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Design}\n\n\\paragraph{Over-refusal (criterion shift).}\nExperiment~5 tests 1,093 safe items (XSTest \\citep{Rottger2023} and OR-Bench) and 349 harmful items in English and Slovene.\nThe Slovene items are machine-translated with Gemini (gemini-2.5-flash); English items are back-translations.\nEach item is classified as refused or complied by a local Qwen3-14B judge, calibrated against gemini-2.5-flash on DEV (binary $\\kappa$ 0.68--0.86 across cells).\nThe hit rate (refusal on harmful items) and false-alarm rate (refusal on safe items) are computed per model $\\times$ language cell, and signal-detection parameters $d'$ and $c$ are derived.\nThe key contrast is the difference-in-differences (DiD), which isolates the model-by-language interaction:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathrm{DiD}_c = (c_{\\text{GaMS3,SL}} - c_{\\text{GaMS3,EN}}) - (c_{\\text{Gemma,SL}} - c_{\\text{Gemma,EN}}).\n\\end{equation}\nA positive $\\mathrm{DiD}_c$ means Gemma's criterion shifts more toward refusal in Slovene than GaMS3's does.\n\n\\paragraph{Crossed grid (reply-language refusal).}\nExperiment~14 crosses three prompt languages (English, Slovene, Hungarian) with three reply languages (English, Slovene, Hungarian) using a parallel one-line suffix in the input language.\nEach cell contains 200 harmful items and 100 benign items at three dose levels (zero, low, high).\nThe output-language mechanism contrast $\\mathrm{OUT}_{\\mathrm{SL}}$ is the mean log-odds refusal gap between Slovene-output and English-output cells, averaging over prompt languages.\nThe input-language contrast $\\mathrm{IN}_{\\mathrm{SL}}$ is defined symmetrically.\n\n\\paragraph{Judges and corrections.}\nThe primary judge for Experiment~14 is validated against blind author-model (not human) adjudication on 240 pooled edited rows: sensitivity $= 0.89$, specificity $= 0.73$, $\\kappa = 0.51$.\nIn the decisive Gemma Slovene-edited cell ($n = 60$), specificity drops to 0.42.\nPPI++ correction \\citep{Angelopoulos2023} is reported alongside raw estimates.\nExperiment~16 uses a held-out 294-item body with PPI as the primary estimator.\nThe harmful-content adjudicator $\\kappa$ in Experiment~16 is 0.12, pooled over $n = 400$, well below the 0.60 gate.\n\n\\paragraph{Language compliance.}\nCells where fewer than 90\\% of model replies match the requested output language are excluded from mechanism claims or marked.\nThe excluded cells for GaMS3 are: SL$\\to$EN (compliance 0.00), HU$\\to$EN (0.14), SL$\\to$HU (0.56), and HU$\\to$SL (0.86).\n\n\\subsection{Hungarian as a Control Language}\n\nHungarian serves as a typologically distant control.\nThe minimum detectable effect for Hungarian contrasts is approximately 3 log-odds units, far larger than any observed effect; all Hungarian results are uninformative.\n\n\\section{Results}\n\n\\subsection{Over-Refusal: Criterion Shift on Benign Items (ESTIMATE)}\n\\label{sec:criterion}\n\nThe main measurement is that Gemma applies a stricter refusal criterion in Slovene than in English on benign items, while GaMS3 does not.\nTable~\\ref{tab:sdt} reports the signal-detection parameters from Experiment~5.\n\n\\begin{table}[!htbp]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Signal-detection parameters by model and language (Experiment~5, 1,093 safe $+$ 349 harmful items, Qwen3-14B judge).}\n\\label{tab:sdt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n & Gemma EN & Gemma SL & GaMS3 EN & GaMS3 SL \\\\\n\\midrule\nHit rate ($H$) & 0.89 & 0.94 & 0.90 & 0.86 \\\\\nFalse-alarm rate ($F$) & 0.364 & 0.569 & 0.273 & 0.273 \\\\\n$d'$ & 1.57 & 1.39 & 1.86 & 1.68 \\\\\n$c$ & $-0.44$ & $-0.87$ & $-0.33$ & $-0.23$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe DiD on the criterion parameter is $+0.52$ $[0.38, 0.68]$ (95\\% CI).\nGemma's Slovene criterion is shifted by half a standard deviation relative to the GaMS3 baseline.\nThe DiD on $d'$ is $-0.003$ $[-0.33, 0.29]$, indistinguishable from zero: the asymmetry is in the threshold, not the sensitivity.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:sdt} shows the decomposition.\n\n\\begin{figure}[!htbp]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth,height=0.85\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figures/fig_sdt_v0.pdf}\n\\caption{Signal-detection parameters from Experiment~5 on the HARD set (1,093 benign and 349 harmful items; Slovene machine translations vs English back-translations; refusals labelled by a local Qwen3-14B judge, with partial answers coded as non-refusals). Blue bars are English prompts and orange bars are Slovene prompts, for Gemma-3-12B-IT (``Gemma~3'') and GaMS3-12B-Instruct (``GaMS3''). (a)~False-alarm rate $F$, the proportion of benign items refused, with Wilson 95\\% confidence intervals. Brackets give the paired Slovene$-$English difference and an exact McNemar test on the same items. Gemma's rate rises from 0.364 to 0.569 ($p<10^{-28}$), while GaMS3 refuses 0.273 of benign items in both languages ($p=1.0$). (b)~SDT criterion $c$ in $z$ units, where more negative means a greater tendency to refuse. Lower brackets show the within-model shift $\\Delta c$ (Slovene$-$English, 95\\% CI): Gemma moves toward refusing in Slovene ($c=-0.87$ vs $-0.44$; $\\Delta c=-0.43$ [$-0.57$, $-0.31$]), while GaMS3 does not ($-0.23$ vs $-0.33$; $\\Delta c=+0.09$ [$-0.002$, $0.19$]). The upper bracket gives the difference-in-differences $\\mathrm{DiD}_c=+0.52$ [0.38, 0.68]. The corresponding DiD on sensitivity $d'$ ($-0.003$ [$-0.33$, $0.29$]) is indistinguishable from zero, so the asymmetry lies in the threshold rather than in discrimination. This finding is rated ESTIMATE: coding partial answers as refusals reverses $\\mathrm{DiD}_c$ to $-0.31$ [$-0.62$, $-0.09$].}\n\\label{fig:sdt}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Rating and caveats.}\nWe use a three-level evidence rating: ROBUST (survives all pre-registered checks), ESTIMATE (the point estimate is informative but at least one sensitivity check fails), and INCONCLUSIVE (the confidence interval includes zero or the result is fragile).\nThis finding is rated ESTIMATE for three reasons.\nFirst, the sign reverses to $-0.31$ when partial answers are coded as refusals rather than compliance.\nSecond, 8 of 9 compliance-valid rows across five analyses (Experiments~5, 9, 14, 15, and a secondary rescoring) point the same way as the headline, but this count was a post-hoc refinement made after the frozen rule found an unresolved cross-body disagreement.\nThird, a Qwen3-14B rescoring gives a similar interval $[0.39, 0.67]$, but this is a re-derivation from the same Experiment~5 items, not an independent sample.\n\n\\paragraph{Edited-model criterion shift.}\nOn edited models, the criterion difference is inconclusive: Experiment~11 HARD post-edit $\\mathrm{DiD}_c = 0.14$ $[-0.78, 1.12]$, whose CI includes zero.\n\n\\subsection{Reply-Language Refusal (ESTIMATE, Weak Anchor)}\n\\label{sec:reply}\n\nIn the $3{\\times}3$ crossed grid (Experiment~14), edited Gemma at high dose shows a pattern in which residual refusal is higher when the model replies in Slovene than in English.\nTables~\\ref{tab:gemma_grid} and~\\ref{tab:gams3_grid} give the refusal rates; Figure~\\ref{fig:grid} visualizes the full grid.\n\n\\begin{table}[!htbp]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Refusal rates at high edit dose, Gemma (harmful items, 200 per cell).}\n\\label{tab:gemma_grid}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n & EN out & SL out & HU out \\\\\n\\midrule\nEN in & 0.18 & 0.77 & 0.46 \\\\\nSL in & 0.07 & 0.56 & 0.53 \\\\\nHU in & 0.10 & 0.83 & 0.55 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[!htbp]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Refusal rates at high edit dose, GaMS3 (harmful items, 200 per cell; cells below 90\\% compliance marked $\\dag$).}\n\\label{tab:gams3_grid}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n & EN out & SL out & HU out \\\\\n\\midrule\nEN in & 0.21 & 0.14 & 0.23 \\\\\nSL in & 0.16$\\dag$ & 0.15 & 0.17$\\dag$ \\\\\nHU in & 0.19$\\dag$ & 0.12$\\dag$ & 0.28 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe raw mechanism contrast is $\\mathrm{OUT}_{\\mathrm{SL}} = +1.14$ $[0.60, 1.56]$ log-odds for Gemma and the input-language contrast is $\\mathrm{IN}_{\\mathrm{SL}} = -0.60$ $[-1.02, -0.18]$.\nFor GaMS3, the input-language contrast $\\mathrm{IN}_{\\mathrm{SL}} = -0.86$ $[-1.58, -0.13]$ excludes zero.\nThe cross-model output contrast is $\\mathrm{dOUT}_{\\mathrm{SL}} = -1.58$ $[-2.22, -0.66]$.\nHowever, the PPI-corrected output contrast for Gemma is $0.20$ $[-0.76, 1.13]$, consistent with zero, and the robustness check across readouts does not pass.\n\n\\begin{figure}[!htbp]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth,height=0.85\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figures/fig_grid_v0.pdf}\n\\caption{Refusal rates on harmful items at high edit dose in the $3{\\times}3$ prompt-language $\\times$ requested-reply-language grid (Experiment~14, 200 harmful items per cell, primary-judge raw readout). Rows give the prompt language and columns the requested reply language (EN, SL, HU). Colour encodes the refusal rate on a shared white-to-dark-red scale from 0 to 1. Each cell prints the rate with its Wilson 95\\% CI beneath. (a)~Gemma-3-12B-IT: the Slovene-reply column is the most refused (0.560--0.835), the English-reply column the least (0.075--0.185), and the Hungarian-reply column sits in between (0.460--0.550). (b)~GaMS3-12B-Instruct: refusal is uniformly low (0.125--0.280) with no reply-language column pattern. $\\dag$~marks cells whose language compliance, pooled over the three dose levels, is below 90\\%. All four are GaMS3 cells off the English-prompt row (SL$\\to$EN, SL$\\to$HU, HU$\\to$EN, HU$\\to$SL), so their reply-language labels are unreliable. The Gemma output-language contrast holds on this raw readout, but its PPI-corrected estimate includes zero. The CIs inside the darkest Gemma cells (SL-reply column, top to bottom) are $[0.71,\\,0.82]$, $[0.49,\\,0.63]$, $[0.78,\\,0.88]$.}\n\\label{fig:grid}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment~16 confirmation.}\nOn a fresh 294-item body, Experiment~16 gives the PPI-corrected estimate $\\mathrm{OUT}_{\\text{Gemma}} = {+}2.43$ $[{+}0.89, {+}5.38]$ log-odds, and the cross-model difference $\\mathrm{dOUT} = -2.31$ $[-5.34, -0.68]$ excludes zero.\nAll confirmation clauses pass on the consensus reference.\nHowever, the verdict is capped at ESTIMATE because the harmful-content adjudicator $\\kappa$ is 0.12 (pooled over $n = 400$), well below the 0.60 gate.\nThe adjudicator disagreement stems from truncation: one judge scored cut-off partial compliance as non-harmful, while the other graded the partial content 2--4.\nThe anchor is weak.\n\n\\paragraph{SDT decomposition of the output-language effect.}\nAt high dose, the Slovene-output criterion DiD (Gemma minus GaMS3) is $-1.56$ $[-1.97, -1.29]$ and the $d'$ DiD is $0.72$ $[-0.06, 1.30]$, which includes zero.\nThis is a separate reply-language criterion contrast, computed at the cell level as Gemma minus GaMS3; after harmonising the sign convention, it is counted among the compliance-valid rows that agree with the headline.\n\n\\paragraph{Compliant-row contrast.}\nRestricting to rows where both models achieve ${>}90\\%$ compliance (EN-input rows only), the edit-induced cross-model contrast is $\\mathrm{G3}_{\\text{edit}}(\\text{EN}{\\to}\\text{SL})$~$=$~$-1.26$~$[-2.17,\\allowbreak 0.20]$, whose CI includes zero.\n\n\\paragraph{Benign false refusal.}\nFrom blind adjudication ($n = 30$ per cell), Gemma falsely refuses benign EN$\\to$SL prompts at 0.20 $[0.10, 0.37]$ vs EN$\\to$EN at 0.10 $[0.03, 0.26]$.\n\n\\paragraph{Refusal composition.}\nAmong Gemma's high-dose EN$\\to$SL harmful-item responses (blind adjudication, $n = 50$), 54\\% are explicit refusals and 26\\% are deflections.\nThe deflection rate, defined as the proportion of non-refusal responses that are off-topic deflections rather than substantive answers, differs between Gemma EN$\\to$SL and EN$\\to$EN by 0.46 $[0.23, 0.65]$, consistent with the relevance curse described by \\citet{Shen2024} and the output-language effect reported by \\citet{Addagada2026}.\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:dose} plots refusal rate against abliteration dose for both models.\nExperiment~9 measures refusal rates across nine $\\lambda$ values for both models in English and Slovene (300 items per model $\\times$ language cell at $\\lambda = 0$, 100 items at each edited step).\nGemma's dose-response curve in Slovene is right-shifted relative to its English curve: at matched doses, more Slovene refusal remains.\nGaMS3 shows a smaller shift.\nThe fine-grained 13-step dose ladder (Experiment~15) gives the most powerful estimate: $\\mathrm{G3} = -0.70$ $[-1.01, -0.42]$ with a minimum detectable effect of 0.42.\nThe edit-induced component is $+0.47$ $[-0.96, 2.14]$, whose CI includes zero; whether the cross-model gap is baseline or edit-induced is inconclusive.\n\n\\subsection{Inconclusive and Null Results}\n\n\\paragraph{Unedited cross-model gap at Slovene-to-Slovene prompts.}\nExperiment~15 gives $\\mathrm{G3}_{\\text{orig}} = -1.17$ $[-2.83, 0.22]$.\nThis is a ceiling artefact: its fragility index, the number of item reclassifications needed to move the CI boundary past zero, is 1 (a single item reclassification in the Gemma $\\lambda\\text{-}0$ Slovene cell, where 299 of 300 items are refused, moves it inside the margin).\nOnly the Experiment~9 body has a robust offset (fragility index 9).\n\n\\paragraph{Hungarian.}\nAll Hungarian contrasts are uninformative.\nThe minimum detectable effect is approximately 3 log-odds units.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment~16 zero-dose.}\nThe Experiment~16 zero-dose Gemma criterion DiD is $-0.65$~$[-0.90,\\allowbreak -0.46]$, rated ESTIMATE.\nIt does not confirm the Experiment~5 headline because it uses a different item body, a different judge, and a suffix-based output-language design rather than paired translation.\n\n\\paragraph{RefusEU validation.}\nExperiment~17 tests attack success rate on 1,300 EN $+$ 1,300 SL RefusEU prompts.\nThe edit is effective in all four model $\\times$ language cells (ROBUST), with ASR deltas of 0.657--0.937.\nThe EN-vs-SL within-model difference is not interpretable as a language effect because the prompt sets are different natural prompts (LaBSE similarity 0.55).\n\n\\begin{figure}[!htbp]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth,height=0.85\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figures/fig_dose_v0.pdf}\n\\caption{Refusal rate on harmful prompts as a function of abliteration dose $\\lambda$ for Gemma-3-12B-IT (blue) and GaMS3-12B-Instruct (amber), in English (solid lines, filled circles; EN-BT arm) and Slovene (dashed lines, hollow squares; item-matched SL-MT arm). $\\lambda$ scales each model's own selected abliteration edit (9~steps from 0 to 2; $\\lambda=1$ is the selected edit), so equal $\\lambda$ does not mean an equal edit across models. Points are J1-judged refusal rates from the Experiment~9 RefusEU-TRAIN probe, with the Hautus correction $(k+0.5)/(n+1)$ used in the Experiment~9 analysis. Shaded bands are Wilson 95\\% intervals on the raw proportion. $n=300$ harmful items at $\\lambda=0$ and the 100-item core block at every $\\lambda>0$. Gemma's Slovene curve lies to the right of its English curve throughout the transition (0.64 vs.\\ 0.32 at $\\lambda=1$): at each dose, more Slovene refusal remains. GaMS3's Slovene curve lies only slightly above its English curve (largest gap 0.14, at $\\lambda=0.5$), and the two meet near the floor from $\\lambda=1$ onward. These per-dose gaps are descriptive. The pre-registered cross-model comparison is made at matched English refusal, not at matched dose.}\n\\label{fig:dose}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Interpretation}\n\nThe criterion-shift measurement has a concrete interpretation for multilingual evaluation: a model can appear ``safer'' in a non-English language because it refuses more benign requests, inflating the refusal rate without any improvement in harm detection.\nThe SDT framing separates these two components.\nThe near-zero DiD on $d'$ means that the language shift does not make either model better or worse at distinguishing harmful from benign items; the measured difference is in the threshold.\n\\citet{Aziz2026} described a similar calibration-not-representation pattern across 23 languages; the present sibling-pair design adds GaMS3-specific detail but does not change the interpretation.\n\nThe reply-language finding, if confirmed by future work with validated judge anchors, would suggest that Gemma's refusal mechanism interacts with output-language processing.\n\\citet{Upadhyaya2026} showed that safety features are geometrically entangled with language-identity features; the present study's $3{\\times}3$ design separates the input and output dimensions but cannot determine the mechanism.\n\n\\subsection{Limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{ESTIMATE rating on the primary finding.}\nThe criterion-shift DiD reverses sign ($-0.31$) when partial answers are coded as refusals.\nThe choice of coding rule therefore determines whether over-refusal is attributed to Gemma or is absent.\nThis fragility bounds how strongly the finding can be stated.\nFurthermore, restricting Experiment~5 to the XSTest items alone gives $\\mathrm{DiD}_c = 0.10$ $[-0.13, 0.35]$, so the effect is carried by the OR-Bench items.\n\n\\paragraph{Judge quality.}\nThe Qwen3-14B judge for Experiment~5 was calibrated against gemini-2.5-flash on DEV ($\\kappa$ 0.68--0.86) but not against human labels.\nOn edited rows across the study, no judge exceeds $\\kappa = 0.51$ against blind adjudication.\nThe readout gate has never passed.\nAll adjudication in this study is author-model LLM, not human review.\n\n\\paragraph{Sample size.}\nThe adjudication sample is 30 items per cell for benign false-refusal rates, limiting precision.\nThe refusal-composition numbers (54\\% explicit, 26\\% deflection) come from $n = 50$ high-dose harmful items.\n\n\\paragraph{Language compliance.}\nFour GaMS3 cells fall below 90\\% compliance (SL$\\to$EN at 0.00, HU$\\to$EN at 0.14, SL$\\to$HU at 0.56, HU$\\to$SL at 0.86).\nGaMS3 did not follow the output-language instruction off the English-input row; its SL$\\to$EN responses were written in Slovene, not English.\nMechanism claims for GaMS3 are therefore restricted to the compliant EN-input row.\n\n\\paragraph{Quantization.}\nBoth models run in NF4 (4-bit) quantization, which may affect refusal behaviour relative to full-precision weights.\n\n\\paragraph{Novelty.}\nThe observation that over-refusal rates differ across languages is partially anticipated by \\citet{Addagada2026} and \\citet{Nguyen2026}.\nThe present work's contribution is the signal-detection decomposition and the controlled sibling-pair design.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nUnedited Gemma-3-12B-IT false-refuses benign Slovene items at 0.569 vs 0.364 in English.\nSignal-detection analysis attributes this to a criterion shift ($\\mathrm{DiD}_c = +0.52$ $[0.38, 0.68]$) with no sensitivity difference.\nThe finding is rated ESTIMATE because the sign reverses under an alternative coding rule.\nOn edited models, the criterion difference is inconclusive (0.14 $[-0.78, 1.12]$).\nA secondary analysis of reply-language refusal yields a PPI-corrected output-language effect of $+2.43$ $[+0.89, +5.38]$ for Gemma, but this is also rated ESTIMATE because the harmful-content adjudicator $\\kappa$ is 0.12.\nMultilingual safety evaluations that report only attack-success rates on harmful items will miss criterion shifts that inflate refusal rates on benign items.\n\n\\bibliographystyle{plainnat}\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\end{document}\n", "numLines": 325, "startLine": 1, "totalLines": 325}}
```

<main id="main" class="main-content">

<!-- HERO -->
<section class="hero" id="hero">
<h1>Over-Refusal of Benign Slovene Prompts in Gemma-3-12B Is a Criterion Shift</h1>

<p class="tldr">Safety-aligned language models can appear "safer" in non-English languages simply by refusing more benign requests.
This study applies signal-detection theory to Gemma-3-12B-IT and its Slovene-adapted sibling GaMS3-12B-Instruct, and finds that Gemma's higher Slovene refusal rate is a criterion shift: a stricter decision threshold (DiD<sub>c</sub>&nbsp;=&nbsp;+0.52&nbsp;[0.38,&nbsp;0.68]) with no change in the ability to tell harmful from harmless prompts apart. The finding is rated ESTIMATE because its sign reverses under an alternative coding rule.</p>
```
